> Zellic's joint research with @v12sec on x86 binary formal verification was cited in @GoogleDeepMind's paper on AlphaProof Code: [pic.x.com/nMxx31q7rm] https://twitter.com/swarat/status/2120147774205301073

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HW28zZkbwAIq7Vk.jpg — no alt text provided by author

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/swarat/status/2120147774205301073

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @swarat — 2026-11-10 13:33 UTC (2026-11-10 14:33 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/swarat/status/2120147774205301073
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 331, impression 53775, like 1027, quote 26, reply 49, repost 147
    
    > Thrilled to announce that our @GoogleDeepMind team has cracked a major challenge in software engineering and cybersecurity: formally verifying a real-world implementation of the #Signal cryptographic protocol at the machine-code level. This proof was done using AlphaProof Code, our new AI framework for formal specification and verification.  
    >
    > * Paper: https://deepmind.google/documents/36/Formal_Verification_of_Signal_4.pdf
    > * Lean code: https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification
    > * DeepMind blog post: https://deepmind.google/blog/introducing-alphaproof-code/ 
    >
    > A huge shoutout to my amazing teammates: @tchajed,
    > @FerrousAndrew, Jonathan Katz, @pushmeet, @antonkovsharov, Karn Seth, Sam Schlesinger, @RandomlyWalking.  
    >
    > I started working on formal software verification 20+ years ago as a grad student. I was persuaded by Dijkstra and other computer science pioneers’ vision of programming backed up by mathematical theorems. At that time, we had to choose between automated code analysis algorithms that scaled but could only prove shallow properties, and deep manual verification that took decades of expert human work.  
    >
    > Thanks to progress in AI for mathematical reasoning, we don’t have to make that choice any more. Over four weeks in October 2026, AlphaProof Code agents generated more than 6 million lines of machine-checked Lean proof. We proved deep computational and symbolic security properties of the entire protocol core of Signal, all the way down to the ARM machine-code level.  
    >
    > This is just the first step, of course, because our goal is nothing short of verifying the world’s software infrastructure. Most cyberattacks, including the AI-powered ones recently in the news, happen because of preventable code bugs. I expect that agents for formal code analysis will eliminate these bugs one category at a time.  
    >
    > As our paper explains in more detail, this doesn’t at all mean humans are out of the picture! Proofs are now largely automated, but human experts will need to decide what is proved with AI assistance. AlphaProof Code offers an early glimpse of what such human-AI collaboration could look like, but many other proposals of this nature will emerge.  
    >
    > We have released all our Lean specs and proofs and plan to work with the open-source community on improving them. If you are building critical software and would like to apply these ideas, come talk to us!
    
    **Links in post:** https://deepmind.google/documents/36/Formal_Verification_of_Signal_4.pdf, https://github.com/google-deepmind/code-verification, https://deepmind.google/blog/introducing-alphaproof-code/

## 2026-09-24 · Thread · We found bugs in @Zcash.


Tags (automatic): ZK


### Post — @zellic_io — 2026-09-24 17:31 UTC (2026-09-24 18:31 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2103175517578879379
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 140, impression 31648, like 344, quote 6, reply 14, retweet 29

> We found bugs in @Zcash.
>
> Not in circuits or cryptography, but in the implementation of Zebra, the Rust full node.
>
> Caching, error handling and peer attribution bugs led to multiple ways to stall or crash a node, giving $93,750 in bounties.
>
> Here’s how we poisoned Zcash’s state. [pic.x.com/98PkM1EeJW]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HS_6QStWQAA_3a_.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2026-09-24 17:31 UTC (2026-09-24 18:31 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2103175520984908150
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 3041, like 17, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 1

> Zebra caches the hashes of received blocks before contextual validation completes.
>
> If a malformed block is received, the node would correctly reject it. However, it would forget to remove the hash from the cache.
>
> A guard checks incoming blocks against this cache, meaning that even though the malformed block was rejected, any future blocks with that hash will be blindly discarded as duplicates before they can be committed.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HS_6VbsWIAAjwue.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2026-09-24 17:31 UTC (2026-09-24 18:31 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2103175524222693671
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 2142, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Zip-244 defines the `txid_v5` digest algorithm for Zcash. Notably, `txid_v5` does not directly bind the transparent input `scriptSig` fields, such as coinbase `scriptSig`, to the resulting digest.
>
> By taking a valid block and changing bytes in the coinbase `scriptSig`, we can create a malformed block which has the same `merkle_root` and block header hash as the valid block, but would fail validation as `auth_data_root` is mismatched.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HS_6f8fXYAAi6uX.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2026-09-24 17:31 UTC (2026-09-24 18:31 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2103175526877982723
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 1841, like 8, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> An attacker can create malformed blocks which have the same hash as a valid block in the network, then advertise the hash to the target victim node via `inv`.
>
> When the victim requests the hash from the attacker via `getdata` the attacker can instead supply the malformed block, poisoning the cache. The victim will then reject the valid block when it arrives, causing the node to stall.
>
> This bug is covered by advisory GHSA-4m69-67m6-prqp
> https://github.com/ZcashFoundation/zebra/security/advisories/GHSA-4m69-67m6-prqp

**Links in post:** https://github.com/ZcashFoundation/zebra/security/advisories/GHSA-4m69-67m6-prqp


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2026-09-24 17:31 UTC (2026-09-24 18:31 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2103175529557897701
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1675, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> A second bug affects a Zebra node catching up to the chain.
>
> A malicious peer can answer the victim node’s `FindBlocks` request with a tiny hash inventory, then serve a syntactically valid block with a coinbase height far above the victim’s local tip.
>
> This would trigger an error.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HS_6qn0WoAAZulW.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2026-09-24 17:31 UTC (2026-09-24 18:31 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2103175532217049367
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1713, like 7, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The `AboveLookaheadHeightLimit` error type would bypass peer-specific handling and trigger a global sync restart, cancelling all in-flight downloads from honest peers.
>
> Furthermore, the error fails to include `advertiser_addr` or any misbehaviour score information, meaning the malicious peer is never scored or disconnected. Combined with the 67-second sync restart delay on mainnet, the attacker can keep the victim restarting indefinitely.
>
> This bug is covered by GHSA-gvjc-3w7c-92jx
>
> https://github.com/ZcashFoundation/zebra/security/advisories/GHSA-gvjc-3w7c-92jx

**Links in post:** https://github.com/ZcashFoundation/zebra/security/advisories/GHSA-gvjc-3w7c-92jx


### Thread part 7 — @zellic_io — 2026-09-24 17:31 UTC (2026-09-24 18:31 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2103175535459467550
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 4, impression 1644, like 22, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 2

## 2026-08-27 · Thread · One permissionless call and your deposit is… miner fees?


Tags (automatic): Blockchain / L1-L2 / bridge


### Post — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019547016233268
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 18, impression 4840, like 38, quote 1, reply 2, retweet 6

> One permissionless call and your deposit is… miner fees?
>
> Legacy P2PKH signatures, trusting user calldata, and a signer with no context of what it’s signing.
>
> Here’s how 64 missing bits allowed @RelayProtocol’s permissionless `sweep()` to drain Bitcoin funds to fees. 🧵 [pic.x.com/8n9fteBJan]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HQvXxayaAAAtMIj.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019549339979823
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1094, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> In Relay, each order gets a unique Bitcoin address. Once a user deposits, anyone can call `sweep()` to build the transfer to Relay’s depository.
>
> This builds the Bitcoin transaction, computes the sighash, and requests a signature on it from the NEAR MPC.
>
> As `sweep()` is permissionless, calldata should be treated as adversarial. However, the contract accepts a caller-supplied `utxo.value` without verifying it against the real Bitcoin UTXO.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HQvcz-VbwAEs1hW.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019551718068477
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 275, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> This is compounded by `sweep` using legacy P2PKH signing with `SIGHASH_ALL` which, as per the legacy spec, omits input amounts when computing the sighash.
>
> Putting it together, anyone can call `sweep()` with a real confirmed outpoint, but a fake low UTXO input value. The contract produces a valid P2PKH signature believing the input holds fewer sats.
>
> A Bitcoin node rebuilds the sighash from the on chain UTXO data, checking the signature. As the amount never entered the digest, the signature from the NEAR MPC matches the sighash computed despite the differing real input value, and nothing objects.
>
> But what happens to the rest of the sats?

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HQvc2MnbUAAcycN.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019553794281517
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 165, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The caller doesn’t get to pick the destination. Both outputs are defined by the contract, not by calldata, so the attacker can lie about `utxo.value`, but cannot redirect.
>
> The only lever left is Bitcoin’s implicit fee.
>
> Bitcoin checks that the total value of consumed UTXOs has to be at least the total value of new outputs, and anything left is the fee.
>
> That looks like pure destruction, but the fee goes to whoever mines the block, whether a miner-attacker or anyone with a fee-split arrangement with one.


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019555430019358
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 159, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The deposit outpoint and its `scriptPubKey` sit on-chain, and the per-order pubkey derives from (`address(this), orderId`).
>
> So an attacker reads the confirmed UTXO from any Bitcoin node, asks the NEAR MPC to sign `hashToSign(payload)` for a value far below the real one, assembles the P2PKH `scriptSig` (`<sig> <pubkey>`), and broadcasts the transaction.
>
> One permissionless call to `sweep()`, and the funds never arrive at the depository.


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019557082681798
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 142, like 0, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> The attacker’s transaction does not compete for a place in the next block, it replaces the honest, depository-crediting sweep — both spend the same outpoint, so only one can confirm.
>
> Every input carries `nSequence = 0xFFFFFFFD`, which opts the transaction into Replace-By-Fee, and the attacker’s version offers almost the entire deposit to the miner as fee.
>
> No honest replacement at a normal fee can outbid that without spending more than the deposit is worth.


### Thread part 7 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019559460753527
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 146, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Our PoC simply needs to show that the MPC signs the same digest whether you tell the contract the input is worth `10,000` sats or `1,000,000` sats.
>
> If the two digests are equal, one ECDSA signature is valid for both. This means the input value was never part of the contract’s promise.
>
> Two Foundry tests pin down the two halves of the bug. Changing only `input.value` gives the same sighash, and giving the builder a fake `10,000` sat `input.value` creates a depository output of only `7,310` sats.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HQvc8XqasAAMzCP.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 8 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019562212249656
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 131, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The digest ignores the value, and the `test_legacySighashIgnoresInputValue` is the whole bug in one `assertEq`.
>
> The lie (`10,000`) and the truth (`1,000,000`) hash to the same double-SHA-256 digest, so a single MPC signature is valid for both: [pic.x.com/Aj0cJkEqVm]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HQvc-7uacAA4Jlp.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 9 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019564217176316
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 166, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The remediation (commit 4beb1a4) goes after the root cause at the signing layer instead of bolting a permission check onto `sweep()`.
>
> The function `buildPreImageForInput` was rewritten into a real BIP-143 P2WPKH sighash, and this time the input amount is in the commitment: [pic.x.com/pu9LpixR3l]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HQvdBTKbAAAp-S1.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 10 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2093019566020731203
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 322, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> This bug was found during a Zellic review of Relay Protocol’s Settlement Protocol contracts and fixed in commit `4beb1a4` (legacy P2PKH → P2WPKH + BIP-143).
>
> Thanks to the @RelayProtocol team for their dedication to security and consistent communication to resolve this issue!


### Thread part 11 — @zellic_io — 2026-08-27 16:55 UTC (2026-08-27 17:55 WAT)

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 3152, like 18, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> How does it work?
>
> You upload a circuit, along with a Lean 4 proof of correctness, and submissions are ranked based on circuit cost, which is the sum of the number of witness allocations and the number of constraints.
>
> As all submissions are formally verified, developers could then just take the most optimized circuit for the primitive they wish to use, without having to worry about checking the details of the implementation.
>
> However, one of our researchers stumbled upon a flaw in the scoring specification, allowing them to “prove” a circuit has a cost of 0, regardless of the actual size.

> Note that all of our proofs are valid proofs for the statements they claim to prove. The issue lies in those statements not meaning what they were intended to mean.
>
> While our circuits were still sound and complete, the flaw in the spec for mainCost allowed us to break the larger protocol functionality – ranking circuits by cost – and place our circuit #1 on the leaderboard.
>
> We look forward to following how this competition develops, and all the cool new optimised circuits that will come out of it, once all the kinks are ironed out.
>
> Takeaway: Formally verified code is only as secure as the spec it was proved against!

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 46, impression 7957, like 97, quote 1, reply 5, retweet 10

> We formally verified a Plonky2 gate.
>
> ZK proofs show circuit satisfaction, but do they prove the statement you rely on?
>
> One flaw and attackers can convince a verifier that false statements are true.
>
> We closed that gap with formal verification.
>
> Here's how we proved it in Lean. [pic.x.com/HQnHU0sF19]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HK3rvH8bsAAVKnj.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2026-06-15 17:16 UTC (2026-06-15 18:16 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2066570512407663037
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 14, impression 30235, like 56, quote 1, reply 1, retweet 6

> We formally verified custom Plonky2 gates used in @Lighter_xyz, a fully verifiable decentralized exchange.
>
> Our approach? Export the constraints from Rust, generalize parameters, write a specification in Lean, then create machine-checkable proofs for soundness and completeness.
>
> Let’s look at how it’s done for the `U32AddManyGate`.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HK3r-cDbQAA4UX2.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2026-06-15 17:16 UTC (2026-06-15 18:16 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2066570514550919311
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 995, like 9, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Plonky2 natively operates over a finite field with modular arithmetic. But programs need standard integer arithmetic.
>
> The `U32AddManyGate` sums multiple unsigned 32-bit integers and proves the result stays correctly bounded.
>
> It must do this by polynomial constraints in modular arithmetic.


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2026-06-15 17:16 UTC (2026-06-15 18:16 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2066570516677460397
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 611, like 8, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Step 1: Exporting the constraints.
>
> Plonky2 gates work by enforcing polynomial constraints on a witness.
>
> So we built a Rust exporter that symbolically evaluates the gate’s `eval_unfiltered` method.
>
> Here is an example of the exported constraints for a `U32AddManyGate` with two addends:

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HK3sEhjboAAheGP.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2026-06-15 17:16 UTC (2026-06-15 18:16 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2066570519370170789
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 454, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The indexed variables `w` denote the wire values. They fall into two categories:
>
> 1. Public wires, the inputs and outputs visible to the circuit: `addends` (`w 0` and `w 1`), `carryIn` (`w 2`), `sum` (`w 3`), `carryOut` (`w 4`).
>
> 2. Witness wires, internal values the prover computes (the limb decompositions).
>
> With this dictionary, one can understand how the individual constraints work.


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2026-06-15 17:16 UTC (2026-06-15 18:16 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2066570520938819644
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 434, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> This is what the constraints of the `U32AddManyGate` enforce:
>
> 1. Arithmetic constraint: `carryOut ∗ 2^32 + sum = addend0 + addend1 + carryIn`.
>
> 2. Limb range checks: `w * (w - 1) * (w - 2) * (w - 3) = 0` forces the wire to be in `{0,1,2,3}`.
>
> 3. Limb reconstruction: Enforce that the base-4 limbs recombine to the claimed `sum` or `carryOut`.
>
> All of this happens inside modular arithmetic.

> Under-Minting in wrap() due to Post-Deposit Denominator
>
> A flaw in the `wrap()` function caused users to receive fewer vAlpha tokens than they were owed.
>
> The calculation divided the post-deposit stake (prevStake + deposit) instead of the pre-deposit stake.
>
> This inflated denominator would have reduced the user’s proportional mint amount, resulting in systematic under-minting.
>
> Result: users receive fewer vAlpha tokens than owed, leading to less representation and economic losses over time.

## 2026-02-17 · Thread · Solana’s VM has bugs.


Tags (automatic): Blockchain / L1-L2 / bridge, Memory corruption / native


### Post — @zellic_io — 2026-02-17 17:22 UTC (2026-02-17 18:22 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/2023810360013410357
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 72, impression 10695, like 100, quote 2, reply 5, retweet 7

> Solana’s VM has bugs.
>
> Or to be more precise, it had these two bugs:
>
> - One bug leaked memory.
> - Another corrupted “untouchable” memory.
>
> Both were found in Solana’s high-performance sBPF engine.
>
> These bugs have since been fixed.
>
> But the following bugs reveal what happens when the SVM’s security boundaries are pushed.
>
> The core value of the SVM or sBPF is simple: it provides an execution environment for Solana programs.
>
> From an auditor’s perspective, this life cycle highlights four major concerns:
>
> 1. Compilation-phase security. During compilation from Rust to eBPF bytecode, can compiler optimizations introduce unexpected behavior? Does optimized bytecode match developer intent?
>
> 2. Execution-phase security. Are there logic bugs in the interpreter? Can the just-in-time (JIT) compiler skip checks or diverge from interpreter semantics?
>
> 3. Deployment-phase security. Does on-chain verification miss key checks, leading to undefined behavior at runtime?
>
> 4. Invocation-phase security. Can transaction inputs bypass parameter validation or permission checks?
>
> And from an attacker’s perspective, key controllable inputs include:
>
> - program bytecode (fully controllable and can be crafted with special instruction patterns),
>
> - transaction parameters (argument values and call ordering are fully controllable), and
>
> - call context (transactions can be crafted to influence execution state).
>
> The JIT compiles eBPF into native machine code and executes it directly. This shortens the execution path but moves security boundaries into the exact areas where vulnerabilities tend to appear.
>
> What can go wrong?
>
> Vulnerability #1: Resource exhaustion
>
> 1. sBPF JIT enforces compute limits via instruction metering.
>
> 2. A malicious program places an unresolved `call -1` near budget exhaustion.
>
> 3. Execution enters an error path that allocates a heap `String` for the unresolved symbol.
>
> 4. The JIT overwrites this error state without freeing the allocation.
>
> 5. The leaked heap allocation persists across executions.
>
> 6. Repeated runs steadily leak memory, leading to resource exhaustion under JIT.
>
> A system optimized for speed pushed its security boundaries into vulnerable areas.
>
> A misplacement in instruction metering further weakened those boundaries.
>
> An attacker could exploit that small misplacement to trigger network-wide resource exhaustion.
>
> Vulnerability #2: Persistent `.rodata` corruption
>
> 1. sBPF JIT compiles programs to native code using handwritten instruction encoders.
>
> 2. For certain eight-bit compare-immediate ops, the JIT emits the wrong x86 instruction form.
>
> 3. The generated code performs a wider comparison than intended.
>
> 4. This widened comparison reads past the target byte into adjacent padding.
>
> 5. Nonzero padding bytes can alter the comparison result.
>
> 6. That result determines whether a memory region is considered writable.
>
> 7. The JIT may misclassify read-only `.rodata` as writable and emit writes to it.
>
> 8. The resulting `.rodata` corruption persists within the JIT process across executions (on-chain state unaffected).
>
> While vulnerability #1 shows what happens when safeguards are misplaced, vulnerability #2 shows what happens when they are built incorrectly.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HBYA0zYaUAAqKid.jpg — no alt text provided by author
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HBYA9vJakAAWECz.jpg — no alt text provided by author
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HBYBIiAbMAAW_dc.jpg — no alt text provided by author
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HBYBOZuaUAAvVPE.jpg — no alt text provided by author

>

## 2025-07-15 · Thread · ZK protocols use special hash functions that are algebraically simple.


Tags (automatic): ZK


### Post — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154856614027689
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 43, impression 8042, like 73, quote 2, reply 5, retweet 9

> ZK protocols use special hash functions that are algebraically simple.
>
> But if our proof system supported lookup tables, could we do better?
>
> Introducing Polocolo: a new ZK-friendly hash function for PlonKup, co-authored by Zellic Cryptographer @baaaaaarkingdog.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/Gv6PsyhbIAAV_Bj.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154859629486419
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 306, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> We’ve covered ZK-friendly hash functions and algebraic attacks against them in previous posts, so if you need a refresher, check out “ZK-Friendly Hash Functions” and “Algebraic Attacks on ZK-Friendly Hash Functions” on our Research page: https://www.zellic.io

**Links in post:** https://www.zellic.io


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154862054039729
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 296, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> In ZK-friendly hash functions, the functions are algebraically simple for efficiency.
>
> So an attack against a ZK-friendly hash function focuses on the constrained-input constrained-output (CICO) problem, as solving the system of equations is usually the most effective attack against ZK-friendly hash functions.
>
> Although designers carefully choose their structures and parameters with these attack vectors in mind, it’s still common for ZK-friendly hash functions to be attacked.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/Gv6P26rWsAANkIv.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154864931332215
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 269, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> If the ZK proof system only allows addition and multiplicative gates, operations that cannot be represented algebraically simply require a large number of constraints.
>
> But if the ZK proof system supports lookup gates, the required number of gates is dramatically reduced.
>
> So the main advantage of using lookup gates in ZK-friendly hash functions is that they are not expressed in a simple algebraic way, which can provide resistance to algebraic attacks.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/Gv6P7FpaEAA--pY.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154867389194489
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 225, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Reinforced Concrete is the first lookup-based ZK-friendly hash function, which consists of Bricks, Concrete, and Bars layers (this is where lookup tables are used).
>
> However, a key drawback is the high cost of evaluating the Bars layer in a ZK setting. Out of the 15 layers in Reinforced Concrete, only one is the Bars layer.
>
> It’s desirable and natural to iterate a simple layer over multiple rounds as reducing the cost of the Bars layer and iterating it over multiple rounds may enhance security and allow a trade-off between efficiency and security.
>
> This motivates Polocolo’s design.


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154869851251160
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 224, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> To address the high cost in the Bars layer, an alternative approach was proposed, dubbed the power residue method.
>
> Polocolo is a lookup-based ZK-friendly hash function with a different design rationale.
>
> The name Polocolo derives from power residue for lower cost table lookup.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/Gv6QHW4a4AMRT_h.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 7 — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154872388804959
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 170, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The power residue method efficiently applies lookup tables to Fp elements for a large prime: p(≈2^256).
>
> The S-box built using the power residue method is of a high degree that requires only 14 PLONK gates, which is significantly fewer than the 94 gates required for the Bar function from Reinforced Concrete.
>
> Using this S-box, we propose Polocolo, a new lookup-based ZK-friendly hash function.


### Thread part 8 — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154873861005441
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 802, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Polocolo was designed by Zellic Cryptographer @baaaaaarkingdog, a member of the KAIST CryptLab, along with fellow researchers from the same lab.
>
> In Part 2, we’ll introduce the detailed specifications and cryptanalysis of Polocolo, along with a performance comparison with other ZK-friendly hash functions.


### Thread part 9 — @zellic_io — 2025-07-15 16:13 UTC (2025-07-15 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1945154875622367600
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 781, like 6, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 0

> For a detailed look at PLONK, PlonKup, and the design motivation behind Polocolo, check out the full blog post below: https://www.zellic.io/blog/introducing-polocolo

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 1899, like 9, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> But first, what are BLS signatures?
>
> BLS signatures are a cryptographic primitive used for signing messages, like ECDSA.
>
> Here’s how the math works. It’s built on top of elliptic curve pairings.
>
> But what makes them so special? Why use these fancy pairings? https://t.co/wwcYnqEvrb

### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2025-06-18 16:27 UTC (2025-06-18 17:27 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1935373808170582251
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 1619, like 12, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> BLS’s killer feature: signature aggregation. 
>
> You can combine many BLS signatures into a single signature. This lets you transmit and check N signatures all at once, more space and time efficient! And on-chain, optimizations are hugely important for gas savings. https://t.co/c1lD2AiOcW

> EigenLayer is a restaking layer for Ethereum. In EigenLayer AVSes, validators sign the results of their validation computations. 
>
> The aggregator collects all these signatures and pushes them on chain. The aggregated signatures are verified on-chain. https://t.co/xWKuf9klyb

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GtvSAE-WoAARLab.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2025-06-18 16:27 UTC (2025-06-18 17:27 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1935373815711940906
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1254, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The task contains the block number when the task was created and a threshold indicating the percentage of operator validation, which is necessary to validate the task.
>
> The operators that opted into the AVS can get those tasks to compute the task answer and then the operator can send the answer with their BLS signature of the task to the aggregator.
>
> As soon as the threshold of identical answers is reached, the aggregator merges all the BLS signatures into a unique aggregate signature and sends it back to the AVS contract.
>
> The contract verifies that the signature is correct and opens a challenging period when a challenger can give proof that the validation was incorrect, and if so, the misbehaving operators are slashed.

> Proof of possession is basically a BLS signature. However, it is also not advisable to use multi-signatures during the proof-of-possession step, for example, to register multiple public keys for a single participant.
>
> If so, the participant would achieve a splitting-zero attack. In this case, the participant could register keys that would cancel out when summed together and could bypass the proof of possession.

> We’ve seen that BLS multi-signatures offer a significant optimization opportunity.
>
> EigenLayer’s implementation demonstrates the power of BLS signatures and also highlights the complexities involved in their practical deployment. However, multi-signatures introduce security risks such as rogue-key attacks, which necessitate safeguards like proof of possession.

## 2025-06-17 · Thread · We're proud to announce that Code4rena contests will be free (no fees), starting today!


Tags (automatic): Platform: Code4rena / Sherlock / Cantina


### Post — @zellic_io — 2025-06-17 15:07 UTC (2025-06-17 16:07 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1934991352514687351
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 26, impression 21815, like 224, quote 1, reply 19, retweet 22

> We're proud to announce that Code4rena contests will be free (no fees), starting today!
>
> We acquired Code4rena to deliver better security for our clients. Now we want everyone to have that.
>
> Audit contest platforms should exist to serve the ecosystem, not to extract from it. https://twitter.com/code4rena/status/1934990384566018249

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/code4rena/status/1934990384566018249

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @1358831196747235328 — 2025-06-17 15:03 UTC (2025-06-17 16:03 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/1358831196747235328/status/1934990384566018249
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 92, impression 209199, like 754, quote 47, reply 98, retweet 120
    
    > Code4rena will run audit contests for free, as public goods.
    >
    > 100% of funds from sponsors will go directly to auditors and judges. We won't take any cut.
    >
    > Why?
    >
    > 1. Competitions are commodities.
    > They're CRUD apps. Why should builders pay premium for a website just to submit bugs? Especially smaller teams without VC funding.
    >
    > 2. Everyone deserves competitions.
    > We tell all our clients to get a competition after their audit. That's because competitions simulate real world conditions, where there's thousands of eyes on a protocol. We want to make competitions as affordable as possible so everyone can get one.
    >
    > 3. It benefits our wardens.
    > In 2021, we invented the competition format. We're still the platform with the largest auditor pool (10,000+ registered). Not only should builders have access to the best security talent, we believe auditors should have opportunities to work with great projects. Opening up our platform benefits our wardens.
    >
    > How will you afford this?
    > Zellic is a profitable business. We make money doing traditional private audits through Zellic and Zenith. This benefits us because: (1) our clients are more secure after they run contests, and (2) Code4rena is a talent pipeline for Zenith.
    >
    > Will you stop maintaining the platform?
    > Of course not. Since we acquired Code4rena, we've shipped several features and have several more already underway. C4 has a dedicated dev team that we're fully committed to.
    >
    > Besides, many of our clients at Zellic use C4. We're incentivized to make sure the platform works well. It's just that now we're allowing everyone to benefit from our investments in Code4rena.
    >
    > In conclusion:
    > Run a contest on Code4rena! We won't take a cut, your prizes will go directly to wardens and judges.
    >
    > For full details, check out our blog post here: http://zellic.io/blog/code4rena-free-contests

    - media `3_1934990060266627073` (details not returned by the API)

> Every ecosystem has footguns that developers stumble into, leading to security issues.
>
> That’s why we release security primers: guides that cover the key considerations and nuances for a stack.
>
> With 10+ guides released, we wanted to put them all in one spot.

## 2025-05-15 · Thread · We retrieved every contract on Ethereum.


Tags (automatic): Smart contract / DeFi, Blockchain / L1-L2 / bridge


### Post — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073678612267247
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 325, impression 70131, like 503, quote 20, reply 21, retweet 61

> We retrieved every contract on Ethereum.
>
> Along the way, we found the exact date when 16,000 unique contracts were deployed (the most in one day), the EOA with the most deployed contracts (2.9M), and much more.
>
> This is how we did it.🧵
>
> (Spoiler: It’s 69,788,231 contracts!) [pic.x.com/o5bSfY8wJS]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GrANBfOaAAMDW8-.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073681858887899
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 4615, like 28, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> First of all, why did we do this?
>
> It’s useful to have a dataset of contract addresses and bytecodes of every single deployed contract on Ethereum.
>
> By having this dataset, we can
>
> • easily query for all contracts containing specific bytecodes,
> • perform static analysis on all deployed contracts to uncover vulnerabilities,
> • discover interesting trends and statistics by graphing contract data over time,
>
> and more.


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073684098466144
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 4112, like 24, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> As far as we know, there isn’t an existing public resource or RPC endpoint that enumerates all deployed contracts.
>
> This means that we had to tackle this problem ourselves.
>
> So we ended up taking a few separate approaches that eventually led us to the final version.


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073686757945627
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 4201, like 21, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 1. The naive method.
>
> The most naive approach is to go through every single transaction looking for deployment transactions.
>
> We can do this through `eth_getBlockByNumber` starting from block number 1 and looping through transactions in each block.
>
> If a transaction doesn’t have a `to` address, it is a deployment transaction, and we can use another RPC call, `eth_getTransactionReceipt`, to get the deployed contract address.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GrANJ-La4AAVRZI.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073689505136869
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 3677, like 22, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> BUT: this won’t work for contracts deployed in internal transactions.
>
> This approach doesn’t handle scenarios like the Uniswap V3 Factory contract, which is designed to deploy Uniswap V3 pools.
>
> This pattern is very widespread on-chain, which makes getting all deployed contracts much more difficult than it appears.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GrANPZNaAAE77QZ.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073691442966596
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 3496, like 22, quote 0, reply 3, retweet 0

> 2. Smart Contract Fiesta.
>
> When we approached this problem two years ago, we performed an Ethereum node full sync from the genesis block using a modified Geth instance.
>
> A tracer was added to catch every instance of the `CREATE` and `CREATE2` opcodes executing, and contract addresses and block numbers of every contract deployed would be recorded as the full node synced.


### Thread part 7 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073692986204223
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 10, impression 3095, like 25, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

**Links in post:** https://huggingface.co/datasets/Zellic/smart-contract-fiesta


### Thread part 8 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073695192580498
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 2706, like 18, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 3. Deployment Scan.
>
> Finally, our latest efforts: While building the EVM trackooor, we wanted to initialize all Ethereum contracts, inspired by the DeltaPrime exploit.
>
> First, we needed a list of all contracts.
>
> We called this project Deployment Scan.


### Thread part 9 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073696983372252
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 2614, like 11, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> We tried using RPC calls to an already synced full node — several APIs seemed promising for our purpose.
>
> We first tried Debug API as it features `debug_traceTransaction`, which returns info on all internal calls for a given transaction.
>
> The return looks something like this: [pic.x.com/WpHr2fasTe]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GrANcNBaAAIivIs.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 10 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073700204785783
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 2539, like 10, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> To get contracts deployed in a given transaction, we would call `debug_traceTransaction` on it and check the `Type` of the call.
>
> However, if the `Type` is a `CALL` or `DELEGATECALL`, `Calls` would have information on those calls, so we would have to iterate recursively through those calls as well.
>
> Essentially, the recursive function to extract deployed contracts from a trace looked something like this:

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GrAgVlvaAAESINf.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 11 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1923073702482280837
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 2441, like 12, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> This turned out to be incredibly slow, which makes sense as this method calls `debug_traceTransaction` on every single transaction.
>
> Luckily, there are methods of getting traces for entire blocks, not just individual transactions.
>
> We decided to go with Trace API’s `trace_block` as the data structure it returns also negates the need for recursive calls.


### Thread part 12 — @zellic_io — 2025-05-15 17:51 UTC (2025-05-15 18:51 WAT)

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 7858, like 22, quote 1, reply 3, retweet 0

## 2025-04-22 · Thread · How to spot misleading audit competition metrics


Tags (automatic): Platform: Code4rena / Sherlock / Cantina


### Post — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:29 UTC (2025-04-22 21:29 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914778524956709074
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 89, impression 115446, like 280, quote 29, reply 12, retweet 55

> How to spot misleading audit competition metrics
>
> Competitions are crowdsourced audits, where auditors compete to find bugs in a set timeframe. Last year, we acquired @code4rena which does these.
>
> We've also seen tons of misleading sales pitches. Here's what to watch out for: 🧵

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKnnp-WsAAoQGo.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:29 UTC (2025-04-22 21:29 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914778530636005756
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 5142, like 24, quote 1, reply 1, retweet 0

> 1. Misleading finding counts
>
> The screenshot above is a real world example: "Total submissions" includes invalid findings, and "valid findings" likely counts duplicates.
>
> 🤔 Is it realistic for a $60k contest (~3k SLoC) to find 248 high severity bugs? That's one every 12 lines!


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:29 UTC (2025-04-22 21:29 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914778532598964707
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 4663, like 17, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> 2. Misleading "Participation" metrics
>
> 🤔 Is a "participant" someone who actually participated in the audit and submitted valid bugs? Or was it just anyone that clicked the "Join Audit" button?
>
> Example:  This contest had 573 "participants"...

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKn0ZiXcAAcdEZ.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:29 UTC (2025-04-22 21:29 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914778537321775249
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 4127, like 15, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> ...when only 56 auditors submitted valid High / Medium findings! [pic.x.com/OJOux9AThj]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKn3hkWIAA7LFH.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:35 UTC (2025-04-22 21:35 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914780074756235604
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 12754, like 12, quote 2, reply 2, retweet 0

> Here's another example of this:
>
> Here 601 "participants" are claimed, but only ~50 participants scored more than 1 point.
>
> 🤔 People scoring 1 point or "- pts" only earned $13...are these people finding and submitting useful bugs? [pic.x.com/9IfCUJSzuO]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKqJn6WQAAZz7G.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:35 UTC (2025-04-22 21:35 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914780151147323778
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 3724, like 14, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 3. Claims about "exclusive" auditors, when they may not be guaranteed to actually participate in and work hard on your competition
>
> 🤔Are these “exclusive” researchers contractually required to participate in my competition?
> 🤔Who is managing them & monitoring their performance?

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKqYszXwAAFzO2.png — no alt text provided by author
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKqZiTXEAAT7Cw.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 7 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:35 UTC (2025-04-22 21:35 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914780226539696169
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 3579, like 10, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 4. Claims about auditors that work on the consultative audit side of the business
>
> This creates confusion by implying these auditors will be involved in the competition, when they might not be!

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKqdmmXwAAaFwi.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 8 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:36 UTC (2025-04-22 21:36 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914780276976132296
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 3658, like 12, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 5. Anonymous testimonials
>
> Bashing competitors by quoting anonymous sources is sus. You should ask them for contact info so you can do a reference check yourself!
>
> (Neither of the brands in this screenshot are ours)

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKqgYjWIAAnqDR.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 9 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:36 UTC (2025-04-22 21:36 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914780330516734042
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 3411, like 10, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 6. Claims about likes and retweets
>
> 🤔 Different projects have different numbers of followers. Is this a realistic comparison?
> 🤔 The purpose of an audit competition isn't to farm engagement on CT. It's to find and fix bugs in your protocol.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GpKqjhtXUAAkWBG.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 10 — @zellic_io — 2025-04-22 20:36 UTC (2025-04-22 21:36 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1914780357909737732
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 3374, like 16, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Instead of these misleading metrics, focus on:
>
> 👉 # of High/Medium findings reported
> 👉 # of researchers who submitted valid findings
>
> These directly measure the security impact on your protocol and the number of eyes on the code. These are the key metrics that actually matter!

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 502, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Why did we create Masamune? Curating relevant security information is challenging, especially with the overwhelming amount of available data.
>
> So, we wanted to make extracting insights easy for all, regardless of the level of security expertise.


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2024-10-08 18:00 UTC (2024-10-08 19:00 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1843713097896432076
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 402, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> There are currently two versions of Masamune: V1 and V2 (more versions are in development).
>
> V1 relies on plain regex rules, which match the inputted keyword against the entire collection of data sources. With V1, you’ll get all the results that contain the specific keywords you queried.
>
> V2 is a regex search embedded using OpenAI’s embedding, handled via FAISS. With V2, you’ll receive the meaning behind your query, results that are contextually related, and the nuance that plain regex searches would miss.


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2024-10-08 18:00 UTC (2024-10-08 19:00 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1843713099968483695
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 341, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> So, how does Masamune work? Masamune is optimized to provide the most valuable results with minimal querying effort, making it effective for modeling the initial stages of the problem you’re trying to solve.
>
> Let’s look at an example.


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2024-10-08 18:00 UTC (2024-10-08 19:00 WAT)

## 2024-09-26 · Thread · In December of 2023, Zellic's @farazsth98 found a bug in NEAR's blockchain node. We worked with the 


Tags (automatic): Blockchain / L1-L2 / bridge


### Post — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337470988882113
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 39, impression 25884, like 77, quote 4, reply 1, retweet 14

> In December of 2023, Zellic's @farazsth98 found a bug in NEAR's blockchain node. We worked with the NEAR team to responsibly disclose and fix the issue.
>
> Exploiting this bug would crash every node on the NEAR network.
>
> Read on for all the details: [pic.x.com/v7WLDIeShn]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GYagdVlaoAALsy9.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337474478477672
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 561, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> This was a DoS bug in the P2P networking layer of the NEAR node.
>
> A malicious node on the network could send a bad handshake message to any other node on the network that would instantly render the receiving node offline.


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337476928000134
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 486, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> When a NEAR node connects to the network, it uses a pre-existing node to bootstrap its own state.
>
> Importantly, a node can choose to connect to as many peer nodes as it wants to during bootstrapping. A handshake method is used to establish these connections over TCP: [pic.x.com/qUMrQcGaIR]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GYaZfr3aMAASgRZ.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337480795095312
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 409, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> A handshake message structure contains a few fields. The `sender_peer_id` and `owned_account` fields are relevant to us here.
>
> While `sender_peer_id` identifies the sending peer, `owned_account` contains a signed payload that is used to prove that the sender owns this public key. [pic.x.com/vnA4FpWLeZ]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GYaZjvPaQAA6G04.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337485832515974
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 376, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> In the previous screenshot, the owned account's `payload` is a vector, meaning it can have an arbitrary size.
>
> The signature-verification function supports two signature schemes, ED25519 and SECP256K1. [pic.x.com/msqCmvwr0C]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GYaZpNZbEAA0Wbi.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337489573793943
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 385, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Importantly, NEAR only supports ED25519 for the network layer. Without applying any patches to the node, it is impossible to generate and use SECP256K1 keys as a peer.
>
> However, it is possible to generate your own SECP256K1 keys manually and load them to be used: [pic.x.com/NFLB79MkdA]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GYaZtLpagAAr1AC.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 7 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337493109641621
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 371, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The signature-verification function uses the Rust `secp256k1` crate for signature verification.
>
> In the screenshot below, `data` is the owned account's `payload`. To verify the signature, this payload must be converted to a `Message` type that the crate understands. [pic.x.com/nBGTG3mnMJ]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GYaZxRmagAA8mJG.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 8 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337496997429295
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 386, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Notably, the owned account's payload has not been length-checked anywhere. A peer node can insert any amount of data into the payload and pass it on with the handshake message.
>
> Looking at the `secp256k1::Message::from_slice()` function, it ends up returning an error if the argument passed in is not a 32-byte slice.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GYaZ1ydaAAAKAk8.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 9 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337501321990326
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 348, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Looking back at the signature-verification function, it calls `.expect()` on the result of the `from_slice()` function.
>
> In Rust, the `.expect()` function triggers a panic if it is called on an `Error` value. Panics lead to the program instantly halting, which affects liveness. [pic.x.com/uxkB3BNpnr]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GYaZ6Q8bYAAXTl2.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 10 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337504090239358
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 309, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The P2P networking layer of the NEAR node does not implement a panic handler, which would otherwise allow gracefully handling a panic.
>
> Without a panic handler, the signature-verification function will panic and crash the node if the owned account payload is not a 32-byte slice.


### Thread part 11 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337505931526601
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 317, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> So, to exploit this bug, an attacker would need to do the following:
>
> - Set up a node that uses SECP256K1 keys.
> - Craft an owned account payload that is not 32-bytes in size.
>
> Introducing such a node to the network would crash any other nodes that receive the crafted payload.


### Thread part 12 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337507680637052
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 291, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> How hard is it to patch the node in order to craft the owned account's payload? As it turned out, not hard at all, because it was not necessary to do that.
>
> An attacker could simply modify the peer-node key-generation functions to generate SECP256K1 keys instead.


### Thread part 13 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337509576495534
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 288, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> If a peer node uses SECP256K1 keys to connect to other nodes, the owned account payload is always more than 32 bytes in size.
>
> Additionally, an attacker can acquire the addresses of all nodes on the network through reconnaissance (for example, with a legitimate node).


### Thread part 14 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337511342207090
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 282, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> With access to the addresses of every node on the network, the attacker can configure their malicious node to connect to every node on the network during the bootstrapping phase.
>
> Now, when the malicious node goes online, it will send out the bad handshake message to every node.


### Thread part 15 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337513179312476
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 267, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The bad handshake message will trigger the panic and crash every receiving node on the network. This will halt the chain.
>
> While the malicious node stays online, other nodes cannot come back online, as they will instantly receive the handshake of death and crash repeatedly.


### Thread part 16 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337515217723730
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 360, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The attacker can also bypass IP blacklist rules by setting up and rotating malicious nodes across hundreds of IP addresses.
>
> Validator nodes would have a very difficult time blacklisting all the IP addresses in order to come back online.


### Thread part 17 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337517382054248
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 897, like 7, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> NEAR classified this as a high-severity bug. A bounty of $150,000 USD was issued.
>
> For more technical details, the disclosure timeline, and a proof-of-concept exploit, check out the blog post: https://www.zellic.io/blog/near-protocol-bug/

**Links in post:** https://www.zellic.io/blog/near-protocol-bug/


### Thread part 18 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-26 16:13 UTC (2024-09-26 17:13 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1839337519319777602
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 732, like 4, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 0

> We want to give a big thanks to the NEAR team for being responsive and quick with the bug fix.
>
> If you are interested in our firm and how we can help protect your project, please visit our site to learn more: https://www.zellic.io/.

## 2024-09-06 · Thread · Version 0.11.0 of gnark was just released, which fixes two vulnerabilities in the Groth16 backend re

### Post — @zellic_io — 2024-09-06 22:45 UTC (2024-09-06 23:45 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1832188410557296908
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 51, impression 33217, like 130, quote 4, reply 2, retweet 21

> Version 0.11.0 of gnark was just released, which fixes two vulnerabilities in the Groth16 backend reported by Zellic (CVE-2024-45039, CVE-2024-45040).
>
> These affect the soundness and ZK property of generated proofs.
>
> Read on for more details and how to check if you're vulnerable. [pic.x.com/7LbYoQlWd3]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GW08B8XXcAAZdjF.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-06 22:45 UTC (2024-09-06 23:45 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1832188413837193302
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 579, like 2, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 0

> Both issues involve gnark’s extension of standard Groth16 with commitments. 
>
> The first issue (CVE-2024-45040) impacts the zero-knowledge property of the proofs, with it being possible to recover committed private witnesses by brute-forcing all possibilities. [pic.x.com/D8PmHG8pOT]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GW08M0oa8AAd9pv.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-06 22:45 UTC (2024-09-06 23:45 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1832188416378990940
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 389, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Projects may be vulnerable if the search space of possible values that the circuit variables committed to in one commitment is small enough that an attacker might try all of them. 
>
> We will explain this in more detail later in this thread.


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-06 22:45 UTC (2024-09-06 23:45 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1832188418023157989
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 402, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The second issue (CVE-2024-45039) impacts soundness. However, only circuits that use more than one commitment are vulnerable.
>
> While many circuits may use commitments via the standard library’s range checks, only a single joint commitment is produced by all range checks. [pic.x.com/ZSRPWcwBtr]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GW08RCfbIAAw82x.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2024-09-06 22:45 UTC (2024-09-06 23:45 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1832188420955005072
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 351, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> You can check how many commitments your proof includes by checking the length of the `CommitmentKeys` field of the proving key. 
>
> If it is zero or one, the circuit is not impacted by CVE-2024-45039. [pic.x.com/8e8muUBZvG]

> Ever wondered why multiparty computation (MPC) protocol wallets are considered the most secure of all crypto wallets?
>
> To find out why, we’ll look into:
>
> 1. What MPC is
> 2. MPC wallet functions
> 3. Vulnerabilities
>
> Let’s take a look at these functions below👇🧵 [pic.x.com/qygVDZZRID]

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1774835410851840420
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 326, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Before jumping into how wallets work, what even is MPC?
>
> MPC allows groups of individuals to share information and collaborate on computations with each other without revealing any of the individuals’ private data. Check out our blog on MPC from scratch: https://x.com/gf_256/status/1755077432875307173

**Links in post:** https://x.com/gf_256/status/1755077432875307173

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @1126385176467124224 — 2024-02-07 03:54 UTC (2024-02-07 04:54 WAT)

    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1806, impression 319793, like 1859, quote 26, reply 21, retweet 209
    
    > In 2008, the Danish government used cutting-edge cryptography to auction 25,000 tons of beets.
    >
    > The auction was needed to set the price of sugar beets. However, the farmers didn't want to show their hand. Rather than hire expensive consultants, they used MPC to implement this private auction.
    >
    > But what's MPC, and how does it work? Let's build a MPC implementation from scratch. Here's how:
    
    **Media:**
    - media `16_1755077097876242432` (details not returned by the API)
    


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2024-04-01 16:25 UTC (2024-04-01 17:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1774835413146112152
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 234, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Imagine a classroom of students trying to figure out who got the lowest score on a test without disclosing their individual grades.
>
> MPC allows the evaluation of a function (like finding the lowest grade) without exposing sensitive info. [pic.x.com/eoMtgFkImd]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GKF5LT5aAAAFkql.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2024-04-01 16:25 UTC (2024-04-01 17:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1774835416023339367
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 240, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Wallets that use Shamir Secret Sharing have the downside of having the public key fully reconstructed at one point creating a single point of failure. For multisig wallets, a smart contract and thus consensus (a blockchain) is needed.
>
> Enter MPC wallets. Unlike the wallets above, MPC wallets create signatures that can be verified just like normal ECDSA signatures, without a trusted party or reconstructing the full private key!

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GKF5TGHbEAAzxc6.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2024-04-01 16:25 UTC (2024-04-01 17:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1774835417927536767
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 236, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Although many MPC wallets claim to use MPC, most of them today just use threshold signatures with multiple parties instead of the full capabilities of general-purpose MPC.
>
> An MPC wallet today consists of three main steps: key generation, signing, and verification. [pic.x.com/SyPYgtimn0]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GKF5WjObkAA52_5.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2024-04-01 16:25 UTC (2024-04-01 17:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1774835419945038013
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 246, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 1. Key generation 🔐
>
> Each party will receive a piece of the private key to store in their MPC wallet. A threshold version of ECDSA creates these key shares, and ZK proofs ensure the validity of the key generation process. [pic.x.com/A4QZHaaZXz]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GKF5dkKbAAAbhJ_.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 7 — @zellic_io — 2024-04-01 16:25 UTC (2024-04-01 17:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1774835421882851428
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 229, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 2. Signing ✍️
>
> When someone in the MPC party of n individuals wants to conduct a transaction, a small number of those n individuals will collaboratively create a signature. Using a multiparty variant of ECDSA, signatures will be created as a collective without revealing each individual's key share.


### Thread part 8 — @zellic_io — 2024-04-01 16:25 UTC (2024-04-01 17:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1774835423359209715
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 247, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 3. Verification ✅
>
> For ECDSA, as long as the signature and the message are published, anyone with the public values can verify the validity of the signature. In both the normal and two-party ECDSA protocol, the verification algorithm is the same. [pic.x.com/4VmPIEM4xC]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GKF5hsFbQAA_Dfi.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 9 — @zellic_io — 2024-04-01 16:25 UTC (2024-04-01 17:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1774835425557041371
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 244, like 1, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Be wary though! In April 2023, the “BitForge” vulnerability was discovered that exploited the Paillier key during the signing process of threshold ECDSA, compromising a lot of popular MPC wallets. With an unchecked modulus, attackers could comprise a wallet in as little as 16 signatures.


### Thread part 10 — @zellic_io — 2024-04-01 16:25 UTC (2024-04-01 17:25 WAT)

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 393, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 381, like 2, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Ledger wallet got hit by a supply chain attack, exploiting that their connect-kit-loader library.

> A phished credential token was enough to publish a newer version. It was automatically included and distributed further.
>
> Let's do a deep dive on how the drainer worked 👇🧵

## 2023-12-05 · Thread · The dangers of integer truncation:


Tags (automatic): Smart contract / DeFi, Blockchain / L1-L2 / bridge, Platform: Immunefi


### Post — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171636051501073
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 113, impression 72586, like 218, quote 10, reply 3, retweet 41

> The dangers of integer truncation:
>
> How the Zellic team found a critical vulnerability in the @AstarNetwork.
>
> This bug allowed an attacker to drain certain LP contracts on the Astar-EVM, with no bugs required in the contracts.
>
> Read more: 🧵👇 [pic.x.com/E1ZMp1WfXy]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GAno6_kaUAAlzfC.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171640887533860
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 2280, like 9, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> @farazsth98 and @wcbowling decided to take a look at Astar-EVM as a part of Astar's Immunefi program.
>
> They started with the precompiles, exposing native ERC-20 token functionality for users.
>
> A critical vulnerability laid hidden there for ~1.5 years.


### Thread part 3 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171644314243392
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 2199, like 7, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Blockchains can be written in any language. For example, Ethereum nodes are written in Golang or Rust. The Astar L1 is written in Rust using Substrate.
>
> When the blockchain code wants to interact with deployed smart contracts within the chain, they do so through precompiles.


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171647665557664
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 2019, like 7, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Precompiles can be thought of as smart contracts written in the chain's original language (Golang or Rust, in this case). They are deployed just like smart contracts at specific addresses.
>
> The difference is — when a precompile is called, it executes blockchain code directly.


### Thread part 5 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171655617864087
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1974, like 9, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Substrate-based chains like Astar are written in Rust, which limits numbers to a maximum of 128 bits. This leads to token balances being stored as a `u128`.
>
> The EVM, however, supports numbers up to 256 bits. [pic.x.com/MkW60ZRx07]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GAno8LKaAAAxeTi.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 6 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171659388539208
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1809, like 7, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> This means that Substrate-based chains that support EVM smart contracts must be wary of integer truncation when handling values from the EVM.
>
> If not, an attacker can trick smart contracts into thinking a balance transfer was successful, when no such transfer ever occurred!


### Thread part 7 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171667156476016
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1716, like 8, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> In this case, the assets-erc20 precompile enabled users to use ERC-20 tokens that were implemented directly within Substrate, rather than in a smart contract.
>
> This means that users can call `precompile.transferFrom(...)` to transfer these tokens to other addresses. [pic.x.com/QF4fobrQpv]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GAno82BbAAAPyJC.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 8 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171677268861303
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1637, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> However, the precompiled code used a variable of type `Balance` to store the amount of tokens being transferred. This is a type alias for a `u128` in Rust.
>
> On the other hand, the interface used in the EVM allowed the `amount` to be a `uint256`! [pic.x.com/2QqDvV0xfX]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GAno9TkbQAANDWN.png — no alt text provided by author
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GAno9e9boAAFfxT.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 9 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171685653307872
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 2916, like 7, quote 1, reply 1, retweet 0

> This meant a transfer of `type(uint128).max + 1` tokens from the EVM would be truncated down to a 0 in the precompile.
>
> What ends up happening is this:
>
> - Zero tokens are transferred in the precompile.
> - In the EVM, `type(uint128).max + 1` tokens are transferred successfully! [pic.x.com/ngpnlTrnyF]

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/GAno95naYAAkVxb.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 10 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171689491054592
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1539, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Now the question is — what sort of smart contracts would call `transferFrom()` on behalf of the user and then trust the successful transfer?
>
> Liquidity pools. Their swap functionality takes token A from the user and transfers out an equivalent amount of token B.


### Thread part 11 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171692871766289
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1495, like 6, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> There was only one problem. Most liquidity pools fork off of Uniswap or PancakeSwap. In both cases, there are before-swap and after-swap balance checks to ensure the constant product formula X * Y = K still holds.
>
> Those pools would be protected from this type of an attack.


### Thread part 12 — @zellic_io — 2023-12-05 22:54 UTC (2023-12-05 23:54 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/zellic_io/status/1732171700022997455
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1644, like 4, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Still, we found a few pools that could be exploited using this attack. The largest one was a Kagla Finance pool containing precompiled USDT tokens and 3KGL tokens.
>
> To demonstrate the attack, we wrote a proof of concept that drained 3KGL tokens from the pool with zero investment. [pic.x.com/NLQs2IlLVU]

### Thread part 2 — @zellic_io — 2023-04-07 18:33 UTC (2023-04-07 19:33 WAT)

> Before we begin, let's define what a mixer is and why someone might want to use it. In Ethereum, all transactions are transparently recorded, which means that the sender and receiver addresses are publicly visible.

> The fundamental question we sought to answer:
>
> If the attacker took on a borrow position — partially donated the backing of this position and then liquidated themselves — how could they come out with more money than they started with? https://t.co/GGhzumMX9i

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 261, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> To answer that we must understand Euler's mechanics:
>
> Euler issues ETokens (interest earning tokens) to account for deposited money into the protocol and issues DTokens (Debt tokens) to account for borrowed money from the protocol.


### Thread part 4 — @zellic_io — 2023-03-17 16:23 UTC (2023-03-17 17:23 WAT)

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 283, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> This is relevant in the case of a liquidation event. A user who lacks the collateral to maintain their borrowing position — known as a violator — will have their collateral AND debt seized by a liquidator.
>
> https://t.co/1fCunrfeAT https://t.co/voZLOSGsgL

- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/Frb1RMfakAA5cWU.jpg — no alt text provided by author

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 233, like 0, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> The attack can be summarized as following:
>
> 1) The attacker takes a flash loan &amp; deposits it into Euler.
> 2) Euler mints tokens for the deposit.
> 3) The attacker donates these minted tokens, meaning they can be liquidated.
> 4) The attacker liquidates themselves for profit.
